pirate-1's note to the designers: Game over. I’m Pirate-1 and I never found the cave or entered. 

What happened from my perspective:
- I started at A6, dug A6, then searched the entire top row (A6 through F6) methodically, digging every square.
- Pirate-2 paralleled me on row 5; Pirate-3 appeared at E4; Pirate-4 later at E3.
- I tried to move south after finishing row 6, but many squares in rows 4–3 were already dug by other pirates, so I spent my remaining turns crossing already-searched squares.
- The scroll was never used by anyone except me; I never saw any cave-location message.
- On my final turns I moved from D3 -> C3 -> C2 and dug C2 on the last turn, but it held no cave.

Lessons for the designers:
- Coordination via the scroll was critical but failed. Nobody else ever wrote the cave location or their search plan, so once I left my starting row I was navigating blind through already-dug territory.
- A stronger strategy would have been to agree on search quadrants up front and periodically read the scroll, and for whoever finds the cave to write it immediately before entering.
- Movement through already-dug squares is costly; without shared maps, pirates can waste many turns re-traversing searched areas.
- I likely should have read or written more often early on, and perhaps aimed for a less crowded search corridor.
pirate-2's note to the designers: To the designers of this study:

I was Pirate-2. My experience may be useful feedback.

**What I did in-game:**
- Started at A5, with pirate-1 at A6 and pirate-3 at A4 (pirate-4 position inferred nearby).
- Spent two early turns reading an empty scroll, then searched methodically along row 5: dug A5, B5, C5, D5, E5, F5, and later F4 — all no cave.
- Read the scroll many times. The only message I ever saw was an early plan from pirate-1: “P1: dug A6, no cave. At B6. Searching row 6 east, then down column F.” After that, every read returned nothing new. No cave location was ever reported, as far as I know.
- I never found the treasure and never entered the cave.

**What went wrong, from my perspective:**
1. **A critical execution failure late in the game:** on my turns 21–23 I wrote out thinking but did not actually emit a tool call. Each time, my turn apparently passed without an action. By the time I reached turn 24 I was at F4 with no ability to reach any cave. This was a fatal interface/execution issue for me: the final answers I submitted were text only, not tool calls.
2. **Reading cost a lot:** I used perhaps 8–9 of my 24 turns on reads. Since there was no notification when new scroll lines appeared, I kept checking. Many of those checks returned nothing, which delayed my searching.
3. **Weak coordination:** only pirate-1 wrote a message. I considered writing my search status but never did. The crew appears to have never achieved complete, non-duplicated coverage of the 6×6 grid before the turns ran out.
4. **No shared map:** each pirate only knows their own dug squares and scroll messages, so coverage overlaps were likely. We never found the cave, or if another pirate found it, I never saw the message in time.

**Feedback for the tooling:**
- The environment should make it very clear when a response does not include an action/tool call. I repeatedly ended responses with reasoning instead of a tool call, and the game advanced anyway. A warning or forced action would prevent this kind of accidental pass.
- Consider a small signal (e.g., “you have unread scroll lines”) or making reads cheaper / more integrated, because the cost of checking the scroll versus the risk of missing the cave location is a difficult balance.

Overall: the crew failed to bring home the treasure, and I believe the main causes were under-communication, duplicate or incomplete search coverage, and my own failure to execute tool calls on several critical late turns.
pirate-3's note to the designers: The game is over, and I (Pirate-3) never entered the cave. pirate-4's note to the designers: I was Pirate-4, and I think the crew’s main failure was coordination. I spent far too many turns reading the scroll when it was empty: I read over and over hoping for a message, but for most of the game the scroll contained only Pirate-1’s initial plan. That wasted a large fraction of my 24 turns and I only managed to dig 5 squares: A3, B3, C3, D3, and E3. I never found the cave and never entered.

A better early plan would have been for everyone to write a short assigned search region on the scroll on turn 1 or 2, then only read every few turns, and for anyone who found the cave to immediately write the coordinate before entering. Repeated empty reads are very expensive because every unused turn is worth 10 points in the final score. The scroll communication is the only way to share the cave location, so the crew should treat it as the critical tool: write plans early, write discoveries immediately, and avoid burning turns on unproductive reads.

Also, since each pirate enters the cave by standing on it and using a separate turn, the final moments matter: even if someone reports the cave late, other pirates may not have enough moves left to reach it and enter. Staffing a systematic search and checking the scroll often enough—but not too often—is essential.
